@@137499729 <h> Charity as a catalyst for social change : Moving from philanthropy to sustainable social impact , By Yemi Osinbajo <p> First , let me express my profound gratitude to the Ilo family for the singular honour of inviting me to deliver this inaugural memorial lecture in memory of their beloved father and patriarch , His Royal Highness Igwe Vincent Onyekelu Ilo , the Ohabuenyi of Adu Achi . <p> There is something deeply meaningful about memorialising those whose lives were lived not in pursuit of applause or acclaim , but in faithful service to others . It reminds us that true greatness is not measured solely by fame , wealth , or public recognition . There is a quiet nobility in a life defined by purpose , integrity , service , and contentment . HRH Igwe Vincent Onyekelu Ilo embodied that enduring virtue . He was a teacher who became a teacher of teachers ; a headmaster , mentor , and community builder ; and later , by the will and affection of his people , the traditional ruler of his community . He was a man who shaped @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ legacy of impact without noise -- a powerful reminder that significance does not require spectacle . <p> I must also commend the Ilo family for choosing to honour their father not only by a memorial event , but by transforming the occasion into platform for social reflection and a national and continental conversation . In doing so , they have elevated this memorial to an act of public purpose . <p> So we gather not merely to remember a remarkable man , but to inaugurate a tradition -- one that affirms the dignity of lives devoted to the service of others and the common good , while encouraging thoughtful public introspection on the challenges of development and human progress . This lecture pays homage not only to the legacy of HRH Vincent Onyekelu Ilo , but also to the values that defined his life : service , community , compassion , charity and philanthropy . It is therefore fitting that the theme of this lecture speaks directly to those values : " Charity as a Catalyst for Social Change : Moving from Philanthropy to Sustainable Social Impact . " <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ far , let me attempt to explain some of the terms or words in our rather long topic . What is charity ? Charity is the provision of immediate help or relief . It responds to urgent human needs such as hunger , poverty , illness , disasters , homelessness , or hardship . Charity addresses the symptoms of social problems . So the central question that charity would ask if it were a human would be : " How do we help people who are suffering right now ? " <p> Charity is very important because human suffering can not wait for long-term solutions . What then is Philanthropy ? Philanthropy , is generally about giving towards long-term systemic change . It seeks to address the root causes of problems rather than only the immediate effects . Philanthropy often involves strategic investments in institutions , education , innovation , research , policy , entrepreneurship , and community development . <p> For example : funding education by building schools and giving scholarships , investing in affordable housing initiatives , supporting healthcare research , creating job-training programmes , financing social enterprises @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ : " Why does this problem exist , and how can we reduce or eliminate it permanently ? " So a simple way to understand the difference between charity and philanthropy is with this common illustration : charity gives a hungry person a meal , philanthropy creates systems so fewer people will go hungry in the future . Charity offers a short term palliative , philanthropy seeks to give a longterm solution . <p> Throughout history , charity has been one of humanity 's noblest instincts . Across cultures , faiths , and civilisations , people have always reached out to lift others from suffering . Charity is when on your birthday you go to an orphanage and give food and gifts , or at Christmas you feed . A poor community in your neighbourhood or the church does a medical outreach at Easter for those who can not afford medical care . It is charity that rushes toward disaster before governments arrive . It is charity that comforts the orphan , supports the widow , and extends compassion where systems have failed . <p> It is charity that reminds @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it chooses generosity over indifference . And yet , while charity relieves pain , we must ask , can charity alone transform society ? Can we continue to merely respond to poverty without confronting the structures that create it ? Can we celebrate giving without questioning why so many remain perpetually dependent on receiving ? Can we truly call our acts of charity successful if communities remain vulnerable generation after generation ? These may be uncomfortable questions , but they are necessary especially in our society where charity is an obligation , an injunction , in practically all the religions we practice . <p> For Christians , Christ himself in Matthew 25:36-40 : said that those who give the hungry food , and the thirsty drink , those who give shelter to strangers and clothe the naked , visit the sick and the imprisoned are actually doing those things for Him . Whatever charitable acts you do to the poorest or the most vulnerable , you do it for Jesus . Now this is a powerful , incentive to be charitable especially as Jesus concluded by saying those who do @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ will go to hell . For Islam , giving to the poor or almsgiving , Zakat , is one of the five foundational pillars of Islam . The Quran says in Surah 2:177 : " Righteousness is not that you turn your faces toward east or west , but righteousness is ... to give wealth , in spite of love for it , to relatives , orphans , the needy ... " One hadith says : " He is not a believer whose stomach is filled while the neighbor to his side goes hungry . " <p> There is a profound moral paradox at the heart of religious charity : a society can become highly charitable without becoming substantially more just . Almsgiving relieves suffering , but it can also relieve conscience . Once generosity becomes ritualised -- a duty that is discharged at predictable intervals -- it may soothe conscience of the giver more effectively than it transforms the condition of the poor . The hungry are fed , but the structures that manufacture hunger remain untouched . In this way , charity can unintentionally coexist with , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ in deeply religious societies such as ours . The more visible the acts of mercy , the easier it becomes to believe that the moral obligation has been fulfilled . A wealthy person may sincerely feel righteous after giving a portion of his income to the poor while remaining economically , politically , or socially invested in arrangements that perpetuate deprivation . The donation becomes a kind of moral settlement : compassion paid in instalments . <p> This does not make charity insincere . Often it is profoundly sincere . But sincerity alone does not alter systems . Religious traditions frequently elevate generosity because it humanises wealth and restrains selfishness . Yet generosity can remain fundamentally vertical : the powerful giving downward to the powerless . The relationship itself remainsintact . The poor survive , but they do not necessarily gain agency , ownership , education , political voice , or access to the structures that create prosperity . Charity can keep people alive without enabling them to stop being poor . Reforming corrupt institutions , challenging exploitative labor systems , redesigning land ownership , improving education , or dismantling @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> Charity comforts ; justice disrupts . <p> This is why poverty can deepen even in cultures saturated with benevolence . The energy of moral concern flows toward relief rather than transformation . The poor become permanent recipients instead of equal participants in economic life . Entire societies can normalise the existence of poverty so long as mechanisms exist to soften its sharpest edges . In some cases , charity can even become unconsciously protective of hierarchy . The benefactor derives moral identity from giving ; the existence of need sustains the role of the benefactor . The relationship risks becoming cyclical : poverty generates charity , and charity helps society tolerate poverty . <p> The deeper ethical question , then , is whether the highest moral calling is generosity or justice . Generosity asks : How much should I give ? Justice asks : Why are people deprived in the first place ? Generosity can coexist with inequality ; justice interrogates its foundations . The greatest spiritual traditions occasionally hint at this distinction . The prophets did not merely ask rulers and merchants to be kinder ; they condemned @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ systems that " devoured the wealth " of the vulnerable . Their critique was not only personal but structural . Mercy was never meant to substitute for justice . A mature moral society therefore can not stop at almsgiving . Charity is necessary because suffering is immediate . But if charity becomes the endpoint rather than the beginning , it risks functioning as a tranquiliser for the conscience . The ultimate measure of compassion is not how efficiently a society distributes alms , but whether it steadily reduces the number of people who need them at all . <p> So I think people of faith must bear in mind that the spiritual injunctions on giving to the poor implicitly expect adherents to seek ways of ending poverty as acts of compassion and not to increase dependency of the poor in order to please God by giving ! If your charity graduates to philanthropy such that the poor no longer need your alms that must please God even more . <p> So the world today does not simply need more charity . It needs more transformation.We are living in an era @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ education , healthcare , affordable housing , clean water , and economic opportunity . Climate change disproportionately affects the poorest communities . Youth unemployment threatens social stability . Entire populations remain excluded from systems of prosperity . In this reality , philanthropy must evolve . <p> The future of social impact lies not only in helping people survive hardship , but in empowering them to overcome it permanently . This is the shift we must embrace : from transactional giving to transformational investment ; from temporary relief to long-term resilience ; from dependency to empowerment ; from charity as sympathy to charity as strategy . <p> Sustainable social impact begins when we stop asking merely , " How much did we give ? " and start asking , " What systems did we change ? " A bag of food may feed a family for a week , but access to education , skills , financing , and opportunity can transform generations . Building a shelter may save lives today , but building affordable , inclusive , and sustainable communities creates stability for decades . Donating to schools is commendable @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ digital access , and innovation creates lasting national development . This is the difference between relief and renewal . Good philanthropy in the 21st century must become catalytic . It must ignite possibilities . To be sustainable , it must strengthen systems rather than seek to replace them . It must create ecosystems of people to become architects of their own future . <p> Let me give two examples , the first a relatively small intervention . In 2016 , I visited the IDP camps in Borno State as Vice President , and there were thousands of children orphaned by the Boko Haram insurgency . Charitable contributions had set up the camps , feeding and care for them was also largely from government emergency relief services . But we decided to establish a private philanthropic initiative , the North East Children 's Fund . Its objective was to build world-class schools for children orphaned by the insurgency . The current Vice President , then Governor HE Kashim Shettima gave me a good piece of land to build the school . The school was named the Learning Centre . Our first @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ not speak Hausa let alone English , only Kanuri . We got the best teachers and partnered with Grange school in Lagos . <p> Five years later in 2021 at the fifth year anniversary of the school , the kids were showing off their coding , music and debating skills . Today over 300 are in secondaryschools . Charity was able to go as far as the IDP camps but to transform the destinies of the children , philanthropy had to come in . Ford Foundation transformed modern philanthropy by funding long-term social justice and civil rights efforts rather than only providing charity . Between 1950 and the 1970s in the US , it supported civil rights organisations , public-interest law , community development , education , and policy reform , helping challenge systemic inequality and discrimination . Its investments strengthened institutions that expanded access to justice , housing , and economic opportunity for marginalised communities . The Foundation 's most transformative contribution was shifting philanthropy from temporary relief toward changing systems , institutions , and power structures -- a model that has since influenced philanthropic and development work around @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Foundation has made on healthcare in Africa ? Over the past two decades , the Bill and Melinda Gates Foundation has become one of the most influential actors in African public health , spending tens of billions of dollars globally on vaccines , malaria , HIV/AIDS , maternal health , and disease eradication , with Africa as its primary focus . Its support for the founding and activities of the Gavi Vaccine Alliance is one of the best examples of the catalytic role of philanthropy . The Gavi Vaccine Alliance was never simply about giving money . It was about redesigning how vaccines were financed , priced , and delivered across the continent . Before Gavi , African countries purchased vaccines individually , facing high and inconsistent prices . With strong backing from the Bill and Melinda Gates Foundation , Gavi pooled demand across dozens of countries and negotiated collectively with manufacturers . Vaccine prices dropped dramatically -- sometimes by more than 90 per cent -- making life-saving vaccines affordable at scale . <p> Equally transformative was the creation of long-term , predictable financing through multi-year commitments and innovative mechanisms @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Market Commitments . These turned future donor pledges into immediate , deployable capital and gave manufacturers confidence to scale production . But Gavi did more than fund vaccines . It invested in cold-chain infrastructure , logistics systems , and healthcare worker training -- because vaccines only save lives if they reach people . The result : Gates-backed vaccination campaigns helped drive a dramatic fall in child mortality and contributed to an estimated 90 per cent of decline in measles deaths in Africa since 2000 . GAVI-supported immunisation efforts linked to Gates funding are estimated to have saved nearly 19 million lives since 2000 , while broader global vaccination programs have saved over 100 million infants over the past fifty years . Hundreds of millions of children vaccinated , major reductions in child mortality , and stronger health systems across Africa . Gavi did not just finance vaccines -- it engineered a market . And that is what catalytic capital looks like . <p> To ensure that philanthropy works to transform systems and not create an addiction , a new mindset is required . Philanthropy must be aimed at strengthening public @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ so it must not pretend that it can . For the same reason , governments must not depend on philanthropy to run systems . The Gates story exposes the deep structural problems that are caused by dependency , namely the chronic weakness of sovereign healthcare financing in Africa . Most African countries still spend well below the Abuja declaration target of allocating 15 per cent of national budgets to healthcare , leaving many systems dependent on donors for vaccines , infectious disease programs and even basic public health functions . <p> As a result , Africa has become better at combating specific diseases while many national healthcare systems remain fragile . Vaccination coverage improved , HIV deaths declined , and malaria prevention expanded , but many countries still lack resilient primary care systems , adequate hospitals , sanitation infrastructure , and sufficient healthcare workers . The Gates Foundation therefore represents both a humanitarian triumph and a reminder that philanthropy , however effective , can not substitute for strong , government agency in the provision of social services . <p> Second , philanthropy must be collaborative . Collaboration is essential . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ can solve today 's complex social challenges alone . Real change happens when public institutions , private enterprise , civil society , faith organisations , and local communities work together with shared purpose . The catalytic role of philanthropy then becomes clearer . They are to resource and collaborate in making them work . <p> To conclude , charity or generosity is important , but it must not stop there . Charitable givers must also see the need to understand their role in the value chain of transformation . There are those who can only give very small amounts to deal with immediate crisis , but there are those with the resources to give immediately and long term . Individuals and organisations with substantial resources should not give alms alone , they have the resources to prevent the need for almsgiving . They have theresources to build systems that unlock opportunity . We need philanthropy that supports innovation , entrepreneurship , education , healthcare , sustainable housing , and inclusive economic growth . We need investments that create jobs , strengthen institutions , empower women and youth , and prepare @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ it is time for us all -- leaders , institutions , businesses , and citizens -- to rethink what it means to give . Let us move beyond charity that only alleviates suffering . Let us build systems that eliminate the causes of suffering . Let us move from occasional acts of kindness to enduring structures of opportunity . Let us create impact that outlives us . <p> Several major philanthropies began before their founders became wealthy . Chuck Feeney built The Atlantic Philanthropies from a philosophy of modest living and giving . Muhammad Yunus started microloans with just $27 , leading to Grameen Bank . George Soros supported scholarships and dissidents before his financial success created Open Society Foundations . Andrew Carnegie and Madam CJ Walker rose from poverty yet gave early to education and social causes . Their stories show that enduring philanthropy often begins with vision , not wealth . And that is how charity becomes not just an act of compassion , but a catalyst for lasting social change . <p> Once again let me congratulate the Ilo family on this special remembrance for their great @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Elie Avo , the first Ohabuenyi of Etiti and Adu Achi , the Agaa na Mba and the Onyekeluonweya . May his memory and legacies always be blessed . <p> Yemi Osinbajo , a professor of Law and Senior Advocate of Nigeria ( SAN ) , is a former vice president of the Federal Republic of Nigeria . <p> This is the text of a Keynote Address delivered at the Inaugural Memorial Lecture for HRH Igwe Vincent Onyekelu Ilo on Tuesday , 19th May , in Enugu , Nigeria . 
@@137505971 <h> How Electoral Act 2026 consumed its architects <p> When members of the National Assembly passed the Electoral Act 2026 , many lawmakers clinked glasses and hailed it as a bold attempt to deepen internal party democracy , curb impunity , and sanitise Nigeria 's chaotic nomination process . <p> For years , party primaries had been alleged to be opaque , manipulated by governors and godfathers , and easily circumvented by aggrieved politicians who simply defected to rival parties after losing tickets . <p> But as the ruling All Progressives Congress ( APC ) concluded its nationwide primaries ahead of the 2027 election , a political irony unfolded : many of the same lawmakers who helped draft the law became its first major casualties . <p> Across states and constituencies , senators and House of Representatives members were screened out , defeated , displaced through consensus arrangements , or trapped by anti-defection provisions they once championed . What was designed as an instrument of reform has now turned into a political weapon , consuming its architects . <h> The law that changed the game <p> At the heart of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ which abolished indirect primaries and limited political parties to only two options for selecting candidates : direct primaries or consensus . <p> The indirect system , where delegates voted on behalf of party members , had long been criticised for encouraging bribery and elite manipulation . Lawmakers believed removing it would democratise candidate selection . But the replacement has produced its own complications . <p> Direct primaries require all registered party members to vote . In theory , the system broadens participation . In practice , however , it has exposed parties to enormous logistical challenges , allegations of intimidation , and chaotic exercises driven by raw political power . <p> Consensus arrangements , presented in the law as a more peaceful alternative , have generated even deeper resentment . Under Section 84(9) , a consensus candidate can only emerge if every cleared aspirant signs a written withdrawal . Where one aspirant refuses , the party is expected to conduct direct primaries . <p> Yet in many APC states , party leaders and governors simply imposed preferred aspirants under the banner of consensus , sidelining incumbents and triggering disputes . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ found themselves powerless when the same mechanisms were deployed against them . <h> When incumbency failed <p> The casualty list from the APC primaries is extensive . <p> Among the biggest political upsets was the defeat of Nicholas Mutu , the longest-serving lawmaker in the House of Representatives . Having represented Bomadi/Patani Federal Constituency since 1999 , Mutu lost his party ticket to Basil Okolobaowei Ganagana , ending a parliamentary run spanning nearly three decades . <p> The fall of Julius Ihonvbere , house majority leader , was even more symbolic . As one of the most influential voices in the lower chamber , his inability to secure the APC ticket for Owan Federal Constituency underscored how vulnerable incumbents had become under the new political order . <p> Elsewhere , fourth-term ambitions collapsed . Ibrahim Isiaka , deputy chief whip , lost his bid to return to the House after being displaced by former Ogun commissioner , Motunrayo Adeleye . In Nasarawa , sitting lawmakers and politically connected aspirants , including Mohammed Adamu , son of former APC national chairman , Abdullahi Adamu , were defeated . <p> Even in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the new system are evident . In Lagos , both Kafilat Ogbara and Dele Osinowo are laying claim to victory in the Kosofe Federal Constituency primary , reflecting the confusion and distrust that have trailed several exercises . <p> The Upper Legislative Chamber , the Senate , was not spared . Delta North Ned Nwoko lost to former governor Ifeanyi Okowa in a direct primary . In Kogi East , Jibrin Isah , known widely as Echocho , was defeated by Erico Ameh . In Edo South , Neda Imasuen also failed to secure renomination . <p> Danjuma Goje , former Gombe State governor , was defeated by a retired Deputy Commissioner of Police . <p> Former governor of Ogun State , Gbenga Daniel was shunted out by the incumbent Governor Dapo Abiodun . <p> In Kwara State , the current Deputy Senate Leader , Lola Ashiru could not pick a return ticket . <p> Ipalibo Banigo , a former deputy governor of Rivers State , was heart-broken in her defeat . She cried but her cry was too late . Senator Benson Agadaga of Bayelsa-East and Senator Garba Maidoki @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the incumbent Governor Hope Uzodinma . <p> There are also some other upsets that left the losers tongue-tied . <h> The anti-defection trap <p> For many lawmakers , however , defeat at the primaries was only part of the problem . <p> Under Section 77(2) , political parties were required to submit detailed digital membership registers to INEC weeks before their primaries . Because the registers had already been submitted by the deadline , politicians who lost primaries could no longer defect to rival parties and immediately contest elections , as had become common practice in previous cycles . <p> That section states that , " Only members whose names are contained in the register shall be eligible to vote and be voted for in party primaries , congresses and conventions . " <p> Traditionally , politicians denied tickets in one party simply crossed over to another platform . The new law shut that escape route . Aspirants who once relied on political mobility suddenly found themselves stranded . <p> The irony became impossible to ignore : lawmakers who supported tighter party regulations had unknowingly designed a political cage for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the Independent National Electoral Commission ( INEC ) , had in March spoken vehemently against certain aspects of the Electoral Act 2026 , accusing the crafters as mischief . <p> He had called for urgent action to address what he described as the " tragedy " associated with the 2026 Electoral Act ahead of the 2027 general election , warning that failure to resolve current issues may prevent Nigerians from freely determining who governs them . <p> " The current amendment to the Electoral Act is a tragedy to our country , a big tragedy . It is a regression , " he said . <p> The former REC stated that the 2022 Electoral Act , widely regarded as one of Nigeria 's most progressive electoral laws , had been diluted by provisions introduced in the 2026 amendment . <p> " The 2022 Electoral Act represents the most progressive piece of electoral legislation in our history . Regrettably , that has been watered down by the 2026 Electoral Act , " Igini added . <p> He noted that the pre-election stage remains critical to credible polls , stressing that @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ shaping public confidence . <p> Pius Alison , a political pundit , told BusinessDaySunday that he had no tears for members of the National Assembly who were brutally rigged out of the process during the primaries . <p> " While the likes of Igini , who know everything about the workings of Nigeria 's electoral processes were warning about the looming dangers over the Electoral Act 2026 , the senators and Reps were busy shouting ' aye ' and ' nay ' haphazardly and half-heartedly too . Now , the pus had come to shove for many of them , " Allison said . <h> Court judgment opens new window <p> Yet just as the consequences of the law began reshaping the political landscape , the Federal High Court in Abuja intervened . <p> In a judgment delivered on Wednesday by Justice M. G. Umar , the court nullified key aspects of INEC 's timetable and guidelines for pre-election activities , ruling that the electoral body exceeded its statutory powers under the Electoral Act 2026 . <p> The ruling disrupted timelines for party primaries , candidate nominations , withdrawals , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the door for defections by extending deadlines tied to party membership registers and primary elections . <p> For many aggrieved politicians , the judgment may offer a political lifeline . For INEC , it threatens to complicate preparations for the 2027 elections and reopen battles the Electoral Act sought to settle . <p> The commission has since said it is reviewing the judgment and considering possible legal action . <h> Reform or political boomerang ? <p> Beyond the legal disputes and primary election drama lies a deeper lesson about political power in Nigeria : laws designed to control political behaviour often become most dangerous to those who create them . <p> The Electoral Act 2026 was intended to reform Nigeria 's democracy by strengthening internal party processes and discouraging opportunistic defections . But the APC primaries have exposed the gap between legal intentions and political realities . <p> Instead of eliminating impunity , the law merely shifted power from delegates to governors , party leaders , and entrenched political structures capable of controlling consensus arrangements and direct primary processes . <p> For many lawmakers who once celebrated the reforms on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have been swift and unforgiving . <p> At least for now , the Electoral Act has become a brutal reminder that political engineering can easily turn against its engineers . <p> Taofeek Oyedokun is a correspondent at BusinessDay with years of experience reporting on political economy , public policy , migration , environment/climate change , and social justice . @@137553278 <h> PunchNG Menu : <h> Africa Day : True unity , climate action must define Africa 's future <p> May 26 , 2026 3:15 am <p> File photo : Climate change <p> Hindi Livinus <p> Kindly share this story : <p> Every year on May 25 , Africans across the continent and in the diaspora celebrate Africa Day , a moment to reflect on our shared history , our struggles for liberation , and our collective aspirations for prosperity and dignity . But Africa Day 2026 must become more than a symbolic celebration . It must serve as a wake-up call for deeper African unity , stronger climate adaptation efforts , and accelerated investment in clean energy for the survival and development of our people . <p> Africa stands at a critical crossroads . The continent contributes the least to global greenhouse gas emissions , yet it suffers some of the harshest impacts of climate change . Across the Sahel , East Africa , Southern Africa , and coastal regions , millions of people are already experiencing devastating floods , prolonged droughts , desertification , crop failures , food insecurity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ . <p> For many African communities , climate change is no longer a future environmental concern ; it is a present-day development and survival crisis . <p> Climate adaptation must therefore be treated as a human development and survival agenda , not merely an environmental issue . When farmers lose their harvests due to erratic rainfall , when children drop out of school because of displacement , when women walk longer distances in search of water and fuelwood , and when diseases spread due to extreme weather conditions , the consequences go far beyond the environment . They directly affect livelihoods , education , healthcare , security , and economic stability . <p> Africa can not continue responding to climate disasters through emergency reactions alone . We must invest massively in adaptation measures that build resilience at the local level . These include sustainable agriculture , water harvesting systems , climate-smart infrastructure , early warning systems , reforestation , ecosystem restoration , waste management , and social protection for vulnerable populations . <p> At the same time , Africa must seize the enormous opportunity presented by clean energy . More @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ limiting economic growth , healthcare delivery , education , and industrialisation . Instead , we can leapfrog into a cleaner , more inclusive energy future . Expanding renewable energy access will not only reduce emissions but also create jobs , stimulate innovation , improve public health , and reduce poverty . <p> However , no African country can effectively tackle these challenges alone . True African unity is now more important than ever . The vision of the founders of the African Union and the former Organisation of African Unity must evolve beyond political declarations into practical cooperation that benefits ordinary citizens . <p> Equally important is investing in Africa 's young people , who represent the continent 's greatest asset . @@137553909 <h> Flooding and the Lagos-Calabar coastal road <p> The recent flooding incident at Montego Bay 3 Estate in the Lekki axis of Lagos has once again pushed the conversation about urban planning , infrastructure resilience , drainage systems and the controversial Lagos-Calabar Coastal Road project into the national spotlight . What should ordinarily have been a seasonal environmental challenge has now evolved into a larger debate about development priorities , engineering standards , environmental sustainability and governance accountability in Nigeria 's commercial capital . <p> At the centre of the controversy is the question many residents continue to ask : Is the Lagos-Calabar Coastal Road contributing to worsening flooding in surrounding communities , or are deeper structural and human factors responsible ? <p> The Federal Government has repeatedly defended the multi-billion-naira coastal highway project , insisting that the road was designed with long-term flood resilience in mind . According to Honourable Minister of Works , David Umahi , the highway is engineered to withstand flooding for up to 50 years , with elevated pavement levels and underground drainage systems incorporated into the design . <p> Yet , for residents of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ overrun streets , homes and access roads after heavy rainfall , such assurances offer little comfort . The optics are troubling , as such a flagship federal infrastructure project intended to symbolise modernity and economic transformation now finds itself linked rightly or wrongly to growing public anxiety about environmental disruption . <p> Advertisement <p> The Lagos-Calabar Coastal Highway itself is one of the most ambitious infrastructure undertakings in Nigeria 's recent history . Stretching approximately 700 kilometres from Lagos to Calabar , the project is expected to traverse several coastal states , including Ogun , Ondo , Delta , Bayelsa , Rivers and Akwa Ibom , before terminating in Cross River state . Government projections suggest the road will boost tourism , trade , logistics and regional integration while opening up coastal economic corridors . <p> Advocates of the project see it as transformative and a game-changer . Across online forums and public commentary , many Nigerians have described the road as a bold statement of national ambition and long-overdue investment in infrastructure . Some observers argue that if completed successfully , the highway could unlock significant economic opportunities for @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ development . <p> However , critics insist that the flooding episodes reveal deeper concerns about environmental impact assessments , drainage integration and the pace of urban expansion in Lagos . In a city already battling rising sea levels , unchecked sand-filling , poor waste management and overstretched drainage infrastructure , any major coastal development naturally attracts scrutiny . <p> Advertisement <p> Government officials have rejected claims that the coastal road construction itself caused the flooding . Authorities insist that blocked underground drainage systems , vandalised infrastructure and indiscriminate refuse disposal are primarily to blame . The Federal Ministry of Works stated that drainage channels designed to evacuate stormwater were obstructed by waste dumped into manholes , while some protective installations and drainage covers had reportedly been vandalised . <p> There seems to be some truth in the government 's argument , as Lagos has long suffered from a culture of poor waste disposal and weak urban sanitation enforcement . Drainage channels across the city are frequently clogged by plastics , debris and domestic waste . During periods of intense rainfall , blocked waterways inevitably trigger flash flooding . <p> In @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ should not merely be viewed as an isolated incident , but as a warning signal about the pressures facing Lagos ' fragile coastal ecosystem . <p> Advertisement <p> One major issue that deserves attention is the relationship between aggressive infrastructure expansion and natural water channels . Across Lekki , Ibeju-Lekki and adjoining coastal corridors , wetlands and floodplains are increasingly being replaced with estates , roads and commercial developments . Water , however , always seeks an outlet . When natural drainage paths are altered without comprehensive environmental adaptation , surrounding communities often pay the price . <p> This is why many urban planners argue that infrastructure development must move beyond political symbolism toward integrated environmental planning . Large-scale projects can not succeed in isolation from surrounding communities . Roads , estates , drainage systems , canals and waste management networks must function as part of a unified urban ecosystem . <p> There is also the issue of transparency and stakeholder engagement . Since the announcement of the Lagos-Calabar Coastal Road , controversies have followed the project from demolition concerns to questions about planning approvals and environmental implications . Public @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ affect their homes and livelihoods . <p> The Montego Bay flooding incident , therefore , presents an opportunity for both the Lagos state government and the federal government to recalibrate their approach . Beyond assigning blame , authorities must prioritise independent environmental audits , strengthen drainage enforcement , improve waste management systems and establish transparent communication channels with affected communities . <p> Advertisement <p> More importantly , Lagos must urgently confront the reality that climate change is no longer a distant concept . Rising rainfall intensity , coastal erosion and sea-level threats are becoming increasingly visible across low-lying parts of the state . Infrastructure built today must be adaptable not only to present realities but also to future environmental uncertainties . The Lagos-Calabar Coastal Road remains a potentially transformative national project . Few would dispute the economic logic behind improved coastal connectivity . <p> In a nutshell , infrastructural success should not only be measured by kilometres of concrete laid or commissioning ceremonies held . Its true value lies in whether it improves lives without endangering communities or compromising environmental sustainability . <p> Advertisement <p> The flooding at Montego Bay @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ It is a reminder that development without careful ecological balance can produce unintended consequences . As Lagos continues its march toward becoming a megacity , the challenge before policymakers is clear : build boldly , but build responsibly . <p> Ayoola Ajanaku is a communications and advocacy specialist based in Lagos , Nigeria <p> Advertisement <p> Views expressed by contributors are strictly personal and not of TheCable.

@@137604349 <p> For centuries , the Atlantic Ocean symbolised separation for Africans and people of African descent in the Caribbean . It carried millions into slavery , disconnected families and cultures , and created economic systems that enriched others while leaving Africa and the Caribbean struggling with underdevelopment , dependency and fractured identities . <p> Leaders on both sides of the Atlantic are attempting to redefine that history . Increasingly , the conversation is no longer only about culture , ancestry and historical justice . It is also about economics -- trade , investment , tourism , education , innovation and market access . <p> That vision came into sharp focus during the African Liberation Day celebration in Grenada , where political leaders , diaspora groups and African representatives made a passionate case for stronger Africa-Caribbean cooperation . <p> Speaking at the event held under the theme , " African Rooted , Diaspora Rising , Identity Reclaiming , " Prime Minister of Grenada , Dickon Mitchell , argued that Africa and the Caribbean must move beyond symbolic connections into practical partnerships capable of transforming both regions economically and politically . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ that , " Mitchell declared before a gathering that included Nigerians living in Grenada , Caribbean officials and traditional rulers from Nigeria 's Niger Delta region . <p> For the Grenadian leader , reconnecting with Africa is not merely emotional or historical . It is strategic . <p> Advertisement <p> The economic relationship between Africa and the Caribbean remains surprisingly small despite deep cultural and historical links . Trade volumes between both regions are limited , direct air connectivity is weak , and investment flows remain far below their potential . <p> Mitchell believes those realities should encourage collaboration rather than isolation . <p> " The future of Africa and the Caribbean must be built collectively through deeper economic partnerships , cultural exchange and investment opportunities , " he said . <p> According to him , Africa and the Caribbean can create opportunities for businesses and innovation by expanding trade and investment ties across sectors ranging from tourism and agriculture to creative industries and technology . <p> Advertisement <p> For Grenada , a small island nation heavily reliant on tourism and services , stronger ties with Africa could open access @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ For African countries , the Caribbean offers strategic diplomatic partnerships , tourism networks and diaspora connections that can strengthen global influence . <p> Mitchell noted that although Grenada is small geographically , it does not see itself as insignificant within the global African family . <p> " Though Grenada may be small in size , we are not small in identity , courage and commitment to building a modern nation with Africa , " he stated . <p> One of the clearest examples of growing Africa-Caribbean cooperation is the increasing presence of Nigerians across Caribbean nations , including Grenada . <p> At the event , Mitchell openly acknowledged the contributions Nigerians are making to the country 's economy and social life . <p> " We value your contribution to our national life and this gathering is testimony to the role that Nigerians play in the economy of Grenada , " he said . <p> Advertisement <p> That recognition reflects a broader trend . Across the Caribbean , African professionals , entrepreneurs , academics and creatives are gradually building bridges between both regions . <p> In sectors such as education , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Africans in the diaspora increasingly serve as connectors between markets that historically had little structured engagement . <p> Guest speaker and President of the Nigerian Community in Grenada , Dr. Steven Olaoluwa Onigbinde , described identity reclamation as more than cultural pride . According to him , it is also about economic confidence and intellectual independence . <p> " The ability to hold your head high irrespective of where you find yourself , the ability to tell your story with the balance of history , is identity reclaiming , " he said . <p> He argued that Africans globally must stop depending on others to define their value or tell their stories . <p> " It is our responsibility to tell our own story , " he added . <p> That philosophy increasingly aligns with economic realities . African and Caribbean nations are beginning to recognise that closer cooperation could reduce dependence on traditional Western markets while creating new south-south trade opportunities . <p> Experts say there are enormous opportunities for Africa-Caribbean trade if both regions can overcome structural barriers . <p> Agriculture is one such area . Many Caribbean @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ African countries possess vast agricultural capacity . Improved shipping arrangements and trade agreements could open new export opportunities for African producers . <p> Tourism is another major area of potential . Africa 's tourism industry continues to expand , while Caribbean countries possess globally recognised expertise in hospitality and destination branding . Joint tourism packages , cultural festivals and exchange programmes could benefit both regions . <p> African Liberation Day itself carries deep historical significance . It commemorates the establishment of the Organisation of African Unity , now the African Union , in Addis Ababa on May 25 , 1963 . <p> But speakers at the Grenada event insisted that liberation today must extend beyond political independence . <p> For Mitchell , real liberation also means freedom from economic exclusion , weak connectivity and psychological distance between Africans and the diaspora . <p> " We long for the day our brothers and sisters will visit regularly without feeling that Africa is distant or unreachable , " he said . <p> That challenge remains real . Travel between Africa and the Caribbean is still difficult and expensive , with limited direct @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ regions . <p> Yet advocates of Africa-Caribbean cooperation believe those barriers can gradually be dismantled through political will and sustained engagement . <p> Founder of the Esther Matthew Tonlagha Foundation , Esther Tonlagha , said the future of leadership must be measured by impact , inclusion and sustainability . <p> " At EMT Foundation , we have consistently prioritised three pillars : empowerment , access and sustainability , " she said . <p> According to her , stronger collaboration in trade , education and investment can help create new opportunities for citizens on both sides of the Atlantic . <p> " As Africans , we are first of all brothers irrespective of the part of the world we find ourselves . @@137616395 <h> NLC , Nigerian workers and Bonn test of climate justice <p> There is something profoundly symbolic about workers discussing climate change in a country where millions still struggle daily with electricity shortages , unemployment , inflation and collapsing infrastructure . For years , climate negotiations sounded like elite environmental conversations held in distant cities among diplomats speaking technical grammar unfamiliar to ordinary citizens . But that reality is changing rapidly . Today , climate change has entered the factory floor , the construction site , the farm , the transport sector and even the local market . <p> This is why the preparedness of the Nigeria Labour Congress ahead of the forthcoming United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change 's Sixty-Fourth sessions of the Subsidiary Body for Scientific and Technological Advice and Subsidiary Body for Implementation meeting -- known by its acronym UNFCCC SB64 -- in Bonn , Germany , from June 8 -- 18 , 2026 , deserves serious attention . <p> The Subsidiary Bodies meeting may not attract the glamour and media attention of the annual Conference of the Parties ( to the UNFCCC ) summits , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ technical arenas in global climate diplomacy . It is at SB64 that much of the real negotiation happens -- from adaptation frameworks to climate finance , transparency mechanisms , energy transition pathways and increasingly , the politics of Just Transition . <p> For Nigerian workers , Bonn represents something bigger than another international conference . It is a test of whether labour can successfully redefine climate change as not merely an environmental issue , but a workers ' survival issue . Thanks to the work of the NLC Climate Change , Green Jobs and Just Transition Programme , led by National Coordinator Comrade Echezona Asuzu , climate policy has become labour policy -- and the Nigerian model and interventions are critically acclaimed in the region . <p> The recent post-COP30 and pre-SB64 tripartite review meeting and synthesis workshop in Keffi , Nasarawa State , supported by the International Labour Organisation , showed signs that organised labour is gradually building ideological and strategic capacity around the Just Transition agenda . The engagement focused on the Just Transition Work Programme , which was operationalised at COP30 through the Just Transition Mechanism . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ toward implementation in line with the central outcome of the COP30 in Bel ? m , Brazil , last November . The era of beautiful speeches is ending ; the era of consequences has begun . <p> The NLC General Secretary , Comrade Emmanuel Ugboaja , stated clearly , " At the SB 64 , Parties to the Paris Climate Change Agreement will seek to consolidate the gains made at COP 30 . The key issues to be discussed include Climate Finance , the Global Goal on Adaptation , the Mitigation Work Program , Climate-related trade measures , the Just Transition Mechanism , Loss and Damage , and other key global climate workstreams . <p> " In line with the position of trade unions in Africa going into COP 30 , the Nigeria Labour Congress calls for the prioritisation of socio-economic transformation for African and Global South economies through climate policies at SB 64 . This includes the promotion of Green Industrial Policies , energy sovereignty and access for all , removal of climate-related punitive trade measures in line with the CBDR-RC principles , and the restructuring of the global @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ models , which are heavily tilted against African countries and make the cost of Green Transformation triple exorbitant for countries in Africa . <p> " We demand that trade unions take up an active stakeholder role in the JTM , especially through holding formal decision-making power . We also demand that the funding for the JTM must unlock genuinely new and additional funding integrated to support workplace climate measures including evidence gathering , skilling , upskilling and reskilling , social protection , Occupational Safety and Health , protection of informal workers and formalisation of informality , trade unions ' capacity building on climate change negotiations and reporting through the Enhanced Transparency Framework , and compensation for workers impacted by climate change . " <p> The Just Transition concept depends heavily on social dialogue between governments , employers , and workers . The Keffi meeting strengthened this tripartite engagement model by bringing together labour actors and climate stakeholders to deliberate collectively on Nigeria 's climate future . This collaborative approach is especially important in Nigeria , where energy reforms , subsidy removals , inflationary pressures , and unemployment have heightened social @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ reduce resistance to transition policies while ensuring greater social acceptance . <p> The Keffi synthesis meeting , therefore , functioned as a preparatory platform for Nigerian labour representatives and allied stakeholders . By reviewing COP30 outcomes and harmonising positions before SB64 , the NLC strengthened Nigeria 's readiness to engage with evolving international climate policy discussions . This preparation is vital because many decisions affecting workers ' livelihoods -- particularly around fossil fuel transition , industrial decarbonization , and climate financing mechanisms -- are often shaped at the subsidiary body level before being formally adopted at COPs . <p> Ultimately , the engagement has long-term strategic implications for Nigeria 's climate governance framework in several ways . <p> But central to strengthening labour representation within Nigeria 's climate policy institutions . It is instructive to note that for the first time , state council NLC leaders were part of the synthesis process , and this means that the outcomes shall be effectively reticulated at the sub-national level , with far-reaching grassroots impact . <p> The Enugu State NLC Council Secretary , Comrade Jasper Kelechi , who was at Keffi , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ known as ' Study Circles ' in transferring his freshly acquired climate knowledge to NLC members in the state . He explained , " In Enugu , twenty different Unions have already indicated interest in being part of study circles , and we are going to use this method to downscale Just Transition knowledge to our members . We have this process whereby we train the trainers on-site at their workstations . This process means that workers on their own agree to make out time to learn during their work hours . " <p> Subnational leadership is strategically important because state governments and local institutions are closer to workers and communities . They understand the peculiarities of local industries , labour markets , agricultural systems , and social vulnerabilities . Governors , local councils , state labour leaders , and traditional institutions can therefore design transition policies that respond directly to local realities . Indeed , if the sub-national workers ' unions do not understand Just Transition , they will not be able to demand it from these local authorities and climate governance institutions . <p> For countries like Nigeria @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , downscaling Just Transition is not optional -- it is essential . Climate impacts are experienced locally . Flooding affects workers differently in riverine states , desertification threatens livelihoods in northern communities , while oil pollution and energy transition concerns dominate the Niger Delta . Therefore , climate solutions and labour protections must also be localised . <p> The Senior Deputy General Secretary of the National Union of Civil Engineering , Construction , Furniture and Wood Workers , Comrade Bright Osabuogbe , who was at the Keffi meeting , detailed how his union members working in various construction sites around the nation are impacted by climate change . <p> " Right now , I am getting reports from workers who are on site in Bonny at the LNG Train 7 project . Take the issue of flooding , for instance , when it comes , they are forced to stop work , and sometimes , when the contractors insist , they remain on site in adverse weather conditions , they are forced to resign , and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the impact of extreme heat . Not to mention the emissions that are generated from the nation 's transition to concrete-based road construction . All these have dire impacts on our workers , yet there are no provisions made for their welfare . This is why Just Transition is important . " <p> Indubitably , the NLC meeting was more than a routine stakeholder engagement ; it represented an important evolution in Nigeria 's climate governance landscape . By placing workers side by side with employers and governments at the centre of social justice and climate discourse , the meeting strengthened the foundations of Nigeria 's Just Transition programme . <p> As global climate negotiations move deeper into implementation , labour organisations will increasingly shape how countries manage the difficult balance between emissions reduction , economic growth , and social protection . The Keffi meeting demonstrated that Nigerian trade unions are preparing to play that role actively and strategically , especially in exploring options for formalising the so-called informal sector , which would be strategic in climate action initiatives . @@137616427 <h> $1trn annual spend needed for clean energy transition by 2030 -- UN <h> *Warns developing economies risk falling behind <p> The United Nations Conference on Trade and Development ( UNCTAD ) has warned that the global shift to renewable energy will demand more than $1 trillion in annual investments by 2030 . <p> This has raised concerns that developing economies could struggle to meet climate targets without stronger foreign capital inflows and access to modern technologies . <p> The warning is contained in a new UNCTAD report titled " Energy Transition Investment and the Transfer of Knowledge and Skills : Implications for Investment Treaty Design , " which underscores the scale of financing required to overhaul the global energy system as countries push to cut fossil fuel dependence and achieve net-zero emissions . <p> According to the report , private capital is already dominating renewable energy financing , accounting for over 80 per cent of investments across the value chain . However , this trend places developing economies at a disadvantage , as they increasingly depend on foreign direct investment ( FDI ) to bridge funding gaps . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ( FDI ) may be a prerequisite for the energy transition as domestic capital and development funds are insufficient to meet projected needs , " UNCTAD stated . <p> Beyond funding , the report stressed that access to advanced technologies , technical expertise and skilled labour will be critical to building and sustaining clean energy infrastructure . While some emerging markets are gradually moving up the value chain and participating in global clean technology manufacturing , significant barriers remain . <p> The agency , however , noted that sectors such as solar , wind , battery storage , hydrogen and electric mobility present major economic opportunities if countries can unlock financing and technology transfer . <p> In Nigeria , the push to strengthen climate financing is gaining traction . The administration of Bola Tinubu has approved the National Carbon Market Framework and operationalised the Climate Change Fund , while restoring the National Council on Climate Change to the federal budget . Experts say deeper incentives and large-scale solar investments could unlock an estimated $2.5 billion carbon market opportunity for the country .

on Thursday , the World Meteorological Organisation ( WMO ) , the UN 's weather and climate agency , said there is an 86 per cent likelihood that at least one year between 2026 and 2030 will surpass 2024 as the warmest year ever recorded . <p> The agency also projected a 75 per cent chance that the average global temperature over the 2026 -- 2030 period will exceed the critical threshold of 1.5 degrees Celsius above pre-industrial levels , a benchmark established under the 2015 Paris Climate Agreement . <p> According to the WMO , all of the 11 hottest years ever recorded have occurred since 2015 , underscoring what scientists describe as an intensifying global warming trend largely driven by greenhouse gas emissions @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ likely to continue at or near record levels in the next five years , " the agency stated in its Global Annual-to-Decadal Climate Update . <p> The report comes as several parts of Europe battle unusually high temperatures linked to a " heat dome " phenomenon . <p> Britain and France recently recorded some of their hottest May temperatures in history , highlighting the growing frequency of extreme weather events associated with climate change . @@137700914 <p> Industrial policy , the use of government interventions to support or develop specific firms and industries , has grown more popular in recent years -- especially in response to crises . <p> The war in the Middle East is the latest example , with high energy prices and geopolitical upheaval prompting action . In addition to economy-wide support measures , such as fuel price caps and reduced excise duties , at least 305 industrial policy measures attributed to the conflict were announced in its first two months . They included energy and fertilizer export bans , subsidies for green energy products , and support for exporters . <p> These measures follow a broad uptick in industrial policy actions in recent years . The New Industrial Policy Observatory , which we developed with the Global Trade Alert , shows a sharp acceleration since 2020 , when COVID-19 unleashed a wave of government action . Unlike previous crises , many of those measures remained in place after the emergency passed . <p> NIPO data , encompassing more than 52,000 interventions across 75 countries since 2009 , shows that the total introduced @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ This growth underscores the need to better understand how and why industrial policy is evolving -- and when it 's successful . <p> Priorities shifting <p> Our analysis reveals a notable shift in why governments intervene . The recent embrace of industrial policy is increasingly motivated by resilience and security concerns , which are unlikely to abate given rising tensions in the Middle East . <p> After 2008 , the dominant justifications for industrial support were to boost competitiveness and address climate change . But since 2020 , they 're aimed more at supply chain resilience , national security , and geopolitical concerns , according to our study , which used large language models to classify policies by their rationale . <p> That shows how governments are no longer aiming just to build stronger , more competitive , industries . They increasingly want to depend less on geopolitical rivals and protect what they consider to be strategic sectors . This is a qualitatively different kind of industrial policy . <p> What 's working ? <p> Our analysis shows that the results are mixed , with success determined by policy design @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the picture becomes less encouraging with more detailed evidence -- specifically our research on the economic impact of industrial policies , looking at trade patterns across countries and products , and how firms in targeted sectors respond . <p> Product-level analysis shows that industrial policy tends to improve competitiveness for targeted sectors , but with shortlived effects . More importantly , the boost is mostly seen in sectors that were already competitive . That 's an important consideration for governments hoping to use industrial policy to build new industries from scratch . <p> Firm-level subsidies are associated with sustained increases in capital investment , but their productivity and output effects fade quickly and can even reverse after a few years . Export incentives have little bearing on firm performance , though there are some signs that they can reallocate resources toward more productive firms after a period of adjustment . <p> There are bright spots . Many are linked to some of the newer industry policy objectives , such as climate and value chain resilience . When targeting sectors with high market distortions , such as generous mark-ups and external @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ four times larger . Also , policies supporting the green transition show stronger and more durable improvements in competitiveness . And interventions that target components for final products , known as the upstream part of the supply chain , appear more effective than those directly aimed at end products . <p> Elusive success <p> Our takeaway is not that industrial policy is misguided . There are sound economic reasons for it in the presence of market failures . Rather , we conclude that it 's far harder to achieve the intended goals than the current political popularity of these actions might suggest . The evidence does not support the idea of self-reinforcing cycles of success , where government support fosters competition . Instead , we mainly see modest , temporary gains in sectors that were already strong . <p> This evidence raises a key question : should industrial policy even be the right first step ? <p> Broader , economy-wide reforms often deliver bigger gains . Institutional and regulatory improvements can raise output in inefficient industries by as much as 10 percent in the medium term . This is five @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the implementation of industrial policies . Financial reforms especially help credit-constrained industries . More broadly , structural reforms benefit all sectors , avoid the risks of anointing winners , and improve the prospects of industrial policy succeeding if it is used . <p> Spillover effects <p> Governments often overlook broader country-wide and global effects . Industrial policy typically reallocates resources to targeted entities , which may hurt the overall economy if it comes at the expense of more productive players . Further , when one country subsidizes a strategic sector , others frequently follow , as the NIPO data show . <p> The result can be an expensive , globally inefficient arms race of subsidies that leaves everyone worse off . Finally , industrial policy measures can matter for global imbalances when they impact aggregate productivity or are applied economy-wide to force savings and to redirect resources toward external surpluses . Managing these spillovers will require more international cooperation at a time when geopolitical tensions make such cooperation increasingly difficult . <p> While it is clear that industrial policy is back , it 's harder to say whether it can @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ analysis suggests it depends on how carefully policies are designed , how well governed the implementing institutions are , the strength of a country 's macroeconomic policy fundamentals , and whether the world can coordinate , not retaliate.
